Interpolation in Proof Theory
Iris van der Giessen, Raheleh Jalali, Roman Kuznets

Abstract
This chapter provides a comprehensive overview of proof-theoretic methods for establishing interpolation properties across a range of logics, including classical, intuitionistic, modal, and substructural logics. Central to the discussion are two foundational techniques: Maehara’s method for Craig interpolation and Pitts’ method for uniform interpolation. The chapter demonstrates how these methods lead to results on the existence of well-behaved proof systems in the contemporary framework of universal proof theory and how they provide a road map for constructing interpolation proofs using modern proof formalisms. The emphasis of the chapter is on constructive, modular, and syntax-driven techniques that illuminate deeper connections between interpolation properties and proof systems.



University of Amsterdam, The Netherlandsi.vandergiessen@uva.nlhttps://orcid.org/0009-0008-4908-2496
University of Bath, UKrahele.jalali@gmail.comhttps://orcid.org/0000-0002-3321-8087
Institute of Computer Science of the Czech Academy of Sciences, Czechiakuznets@cs.cas.czhttps://orcid.org/0000-0001-5894-8724

Contents

1
Introduction

Proof-theoretic techniques towards interpolation originating from the 1950/60’s
[
19
,
60
]
are well-established and play an important role in the contemporary research on interpolation. The aim of this chapter is to provide a nice balance between (1) the
basics
of the praised proof-theoretic methods based on sequent calculi to prove interpolation properties (IPs), (2) the recent
applications
of such methods to the existence of proof systems within the emerging field of
universal proof theory
, and (3) the
development
of similar methods for more powerful contemporary proof systems such as labelled sequents.

The proof-theoretic method to prove the Craig interpolation property (CIP) is known as Maehara’s method
[
60
]
. It provides an algorithm that, given a sequent proof of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
, produces a Craig interpolant
\theta
for it. This method is
constructive
: it explicitly constructs an interpolant, instead of merely proving its existence. Sometimes, the constructed interpolants preserve the polarity of propositional variables, which then shows the so-called Lyndon interpolation property (LIP). Maehara’s method is used for a wide range of logics, including classical and intuitionistic first-order logic
[
80
,
81
,
77
]
, (intuitionistic) modal logic
[
26
,
87
]
, linear logic
[
75
]
, and substructural logic
[
69
]
.

Pitts
[
72
]
has developed a syntactic method to prove the uniform interpolation property (UIP) for the intuitionistic propositional logic
{\sf IPC}
(see
Chapter 9
, the main chapter on uniform interpolation). At the time, this was a rather surprising result as it shows that propositional quantification can be interpreted in
{\sf IPC}
itself. Pitts uses a strongly terminating sequent calculus for
{\sf IPC}
to prove the UIP. Pitts’ idea was generalized to various propositional and (intuitionistic) modal logics
[
11
,
42
,
41
,
4
,
23
]
and formalized in Coq/Rocq
[
24
,
23
]
enabling the automatic computation of uniform interpolants.

Maehara’s method and Pitts’ method are based on sequent calculi. With the development of more expressive sequent structures, such as labelled sequents, hypersequents, and nested sequents, related methods were designed to prove interpolation properties using such structures
[
51
,
54
,
59
,
86
,
85
]
. The new methods reveal that a more robust type of ‘interpolants’ is needed where it does not suffice to define them as formulas, but rather as structures that mimic the type of sequent structure at hand. Interestingly, such calculi seem more suitable to prove the LIP than Maehara’s method via analytic sequent calculi.

The proof-theoretic approach to interpolation is not only useful in light of its constructive nature. The approach can also establish general results in proof theory itself. In the field of
universal proof theory
, structural conditions on sequent rules are identified that guarantee the CIP or UIP
[
42
,
7
,
5
,
4
]
. Such sequent rules are called
semi-analytic
and capture the intuitive notion of ‘nice’
rules. Given the fact that interpolation properties are rare among intermediate logics, most such logics cannot have such ‘nice’ sequent calculi.

The advantages of the proof-theoretic method are the following (we already mentioned a couple): it provides a
constructive
proof of interpolation properties; the complexity of a Craig interpolant is often
linear
in the size of the proof; it is flexible across different classes of logics; and it finds applications in
proof complexity
and
universal proof theory
. There are also some disadvantages.
Although the method is
constructive
, it does not immediately provide an algorithm for computing
an interpolant based on a provable implication
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
because it requires the construction of a sequent proof first. Also, Maehara’s method is not complete meaning that it might be that given
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
some of its interpolants cannot be constructed via this method
[
38
]
.

Some applications and developments of the proof-theoretic approach to interpolation can be found in other chapters of this book.
Chapter 6
on
proof complexity
discusses the feasible extraction of interpolants from proofs, which relates to the NP vs. coNP problem.
Chapter 11
discusses new Maehara-type methods for
cyclic proofs
.

This chapter is structured as follows.
We choose to illustrate the methods for the simple propositional and modal logics introduced in
\cref
subseq:prel. The basics of Maehara’s method and Pitts’ method are explained in
\cref
sec:basics. The modularization of these methods and its impact on universal proof theory is discussed in
\cref
sec:universal-proof-theory. In
\cref
sect:gener the proof-theoretic method and results for labelled sequents, hypersequents, and nested sequents is discussed. In
\cref
sec:conclusion we provide some concluding remarks.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Propositional and Modal Logic

Fix a countable set of
propositional variables

\mathsf{Prop}=\{p,q,\dots\}
, also called
atoms
.
For propositional logic we work with the language
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\bot,\top,\vee,\wedge,\rightarrow\}
and use symbols
\varphi,\psi,\dots
to denote formulas generated from
\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
as usual.
The set of all formulas is denoted by
{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}}
.
We use the standard abbreviation
\neg\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi\rightarrow\bot
.
The base logics used in this chapter are
classical propositional logic

{\sf CPC}
and
intuitionistic propositional logic

{\sf IPC}
, where
{\sf IPC}
can be defined as
{\sf CPC}
without the law of the excluded middle
\varphi\vee\neg\varphi
.
An
intermediate logic
is any logic
L
such that
{\sf IPC}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}\subseteq{\sf CPC}
, where we consider a logic as a set of formulas closed under substitution and modus ponens.
The interested reader is referred to
[
82
,
81
]
.

For modal logic we work with the language
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}\cup\{\Box\}
.
As usual we define
\Diamond\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\neg\Box\neg\varphi
.
We define
\Box^{n}\varphi
inductively as
\Box^{0}\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varphi
and
\Box^{n+1}\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Box(\Box^{n}\varphi)
.
\Cref
fig:modal-axioms presents standard modal axioms and rules, and the left column of
\cref
fig:modal-logics presents common modal logics.
Semantically, modal logics can be characterized by Kripke frames, also known as
linear transition systems
.
A
Kripke model
is a tuple
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
with a non-empty set
W
of
worlds
, a binary relation
R\subseteq W\times W
called the
accessibility relation
, and a function
V\colon W\to\mathcal{P}(\mathsf{Prop})
called a
valuation
.

\Cref
fig:modal_satisfaction provides the inductive definition of the
modal satisfaction relation

\models
between worlds in a model and formulas.
We say that formula
\varphi
is
valid
in model
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
iff
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi
for all worlds
w\in W
.
Well-known soundness and completeness results are summarized in
\cref
fig:modal-logics, where
\varphi
is in a logic whenever it is valid in all respective models of the indicated classes.
We refer to
[
14
,
18
]
for full introductions on modal logic.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\textnormal{({k})}&\Box(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)\rightarrow(\Box\varphi\rightarrow\Box\psi)\\
\textnormal{({t})}&\Box\varphi\rightarrow\varphi\\
\textnormal{({4})}&\Box\varphi\rightarrow\Box\Box\varphi\\
\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}\textnormal{({5})}&\neg\Box\varphi\rightarrow\Box\neg\Box\varphi\\
\textnormal{({d})}&\Box\varphi\rightarrow\Diamond\varphi\\
\textnormal{({wl\"{o}b})}&\Box(\Box\varphi\rightarrow\varphi)\rightarrow\Box\varphi\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}{\textnormal{({MP})}}&{{}{}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\varphi\rightarrow\psi$}}\kern 10.00002pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\varphi$}}}}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to40.15271pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\kern 16.8194pt\hbox{\hbox{$\psi$}}\kern 16.8194pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\\
{\textnormal{({Nec})}}&{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 3.8889pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\varphi$}}}\kern 3.8889pt}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to14.31947pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.5pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.43518pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Box\varphi$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}\end{array}

Figure 1:
Modal axioms and the inference rules modus ponens (
MP
) and necessitation (
Nec
).

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathcal{M},w\models p&\text{if{f} }&p\in V(w);\\
\mathcal{M},w\models\top;&&\\
\mathcal{M},w\not\models\bot;&&\\
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\rightarrow\psi&\text{if{f} }&\mathcal{M},w\not\models\varphi\text{ or }\mathcal{M},w\models\psi;\\
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\vee\psi&\text{if{f} }&\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\text{ or }\mathcal{M},w\models\psi;\\
\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\wedge\psi&\text{if{f} }&\mathcal{M},w\models\varphi\text{ and }\mathcal{M},w\models\psi;\\
\mathcal{M},w\models\Box\varphi&\text{if{f} }&\text{for all }v\text{ such that }wRv,\text{ we have }\mathcal{M},v\models\varphi.\end{array}

Definition of the modal satisfaction relation
\models
between Kripke model
\mathcal{M}=(W,R,V)
, world
w\in W
, and formula
\varphi
.

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\sf CPC}+\textnormal{({k})}

class of all models

T

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({t})}

class of all reflexive models

D

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({d})}

class of all serial models

K4

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({4})}

class of all transitive models

S4

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({t})}+\textnormal{({4})}

class of all transitive and reflexive models

S5

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({t})}+\textnormal{({5})}

class of all total models

GL

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}+\textnormal{({wl\"{o}b})}

class of all transitive and conversely well-founded models

Figure 3:
Axiomatizations of classical modal logics (each should be understood as closed under rules (
MP
) and (
Nec
)) with their soundness and completeness results.

In general, a language
\mathcal{L}
is a set of symbols and we write
{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
to denote the set of formulas generated from
\mathsf{Prop}
and
\mathcal{L}
. For every language, we use symbols
\varphi,\psi,\dots
to denote formulas. We write
\mathsf{Sub}(\varphi)
for the set of all subformulas occurring in formula
\varphi
. For a logic
L
, we write
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi
to mean
\varphi\in\textnormal{{L}}
.

2.2
Sequent Calculi

Sequent calculi are rule-based descriptions of a logic and originate from Gentzen
[
32
,
33
]
. Here we introduce sequent calculi for propositional logic and modal logic
[
32
,
80
,
77
,
81
]
.

A
sequent
is an expression of the form
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
where
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite multisets of formulas.

\Gamma
is called the
antecedent
of such a sequent, and
\Delta
is called its
succedent
or
conclusion
. If
\Delta
contains at most one formula, the sequent is called a
single-conclusion
sequent. We use capital letters
S,S^{\prime},S_{1},S_{2},\dots
to refer to sequents. We use the notation
\Gamma,\varphi
for the multiset
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
and
\Gamma,\Delta
for
\Gamma\cup\Delta
. We define
\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\mathsf{Sub}(\varphi)
for the set of all subformulas of formulas occurring in
\Gamma
and
\Box\Gamma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\Box\varphi\mid\varphi\in\Gamma\}
.

The intended meaning of a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is the formula

\bigwedge_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\varphi\rightarrow\bigvee_{\psi\in\Delta}\psi

also called the
formula interpretation
of the sequent. We adopt the convention that the empty conjunction is
\top
and the empty disjunction is
\bot
.

An
inference rule
is an expression of the form on the left below where
S
and
S_{i}
’s are sequents. Each inference rule gives rise to different
rule instances
, namely for every substitution
\sigma
, the expression on the right is an
instance
of the rule.

S_{1}

S_{2}

\dots

S_{n}

S

\sigma{S_{1}}

\sigma{S_{2}}

\dots

\sigma{S_{n}}

\sigma{S}

(1)

This is a rather formal way of presenting sequent rules needed in
\cref
sec:semi-analytic,
but for now we adopt a standard practice in proof theory of leaving the substitutions implicit and write rules as
rule schemas
(e.g.,
\cref
fig:G3pc), in which so-called
multiset variables
(resp. formula variables and atom variables) in the rules can represent any multiset (resp. formula and atom).

The upper sequent(s) of a rule are called the
premise(s)
and the lower sequent is called the
conclusion
. A rule with no premises is called an
axiom
, a rule with one premise is a
unary
rule, and a rule with two premises is a
binary
rule. When considering rule schemas as in
\cref
fig:G3pc, the distinguished formula in the conclusion is called the
principal formula
and the multisets like
\Gamma,\Delta
, etc. are called the
context
. The formulas in the premises that are not in the context are called the
active
formulas of the rule. Axiom
(\mathsf{id})
differs in that it has two principal formulas but no active ones. In the cut rule (
\cref
fig:G3pc) we call
\varphi
the
cut formula
.

A
sequent calculus
is a finite set of inference rules and a
proof
in a given sequent calculus
\mathbf{G}
is a finite rooted tree in which the nodes are sequents generated inductively from instances of the inference rules in
\mathbf{G}
. This means that in a proof all leaves are instances of axioms. The root of the tree is called the
endsequent
and is the sequent proved by the sequent proof.
We write
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
iff there is a proof in sequent calculus
\mathbf{G}
with endsequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
.

Sequent calculi are used to represent the provable formulas of a logic via soundness and completeness theorems. This is reflected in the following definition:

Definition 1
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a sequent calculus and let
L
be a logic over the same language
\mathcal{L}
. We say that
\mathbf{G}
is a
sequent calculus for

L
, or that
L
is
the logic of

\mathbf{G}
, iff for every sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
, we have
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
iff
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\bigwedge\Gamma\rightarrow\bigvee\Delta
.

\Cref

fig:G3pc defines the well-known sequent calculus
{\mathbf{LK}}
for classical logic
{\sf CPC}
and the single-conclusion calculus
{\mathbf{LJ}}
for intuitionistic logic
{\sf IPC}
. We define the calculi without the cut rule (
\cref
fig:G3pc).
\Cref
fig:modal-rules presents some well-known modal rules, and
\cref
fig:modal-sequent defines calculi for classical modal logics.
We denote sequent calculi in bold font, using the name of the logic, sometimes prefixed with
\mathbf{G}\mathbf{.}
, to identify the system. Thus,
in
\cref
fig:modal-rules we mean that
{\mathbf{G.K}}
is a sequent calculus for modal logic
K
, etc.

\scriptstyle\mathsf{id}\;

p\Rightarrow p

\scriptstyle\bot_{l}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{l}\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\vee_{l}\;

\varphi\vee\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\varphi_{i},\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\wedge_{l}^{i}\;

\;{(i=0,1)}

\varphi_{0}\wedge\varphi_{1},\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\top_{r}\;

\Rightarrow\top

\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\psi

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{r}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi_{i}

\scriptstyle\vee_{r}^{i}\;

\;{(i=0,1)}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi_{0}\vee\varphi_{1}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\psi

\scriptstyle\wedge_{r}\;

\scriptstyle\mathsf{w}_{l}\;

\varphi,\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}_{l}\;

\scriptstyle\mathsf{cut}\;

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{LK}}
. Subscripts
l
and
r
stand for
left
and
right
rule respectively. The
structural rules
are
weakening

(\mathsf{w}_{l})
and
(\mathsf{w}_{r})
and
contraction

(\mathsf{c}_{l})
and
(\mathsf{c}_{r})
. Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{LJ}}
is obtained by restricting these rules to single-conclusion sequents, i.e.,
\Delta=\varnothing
in the right rules, and in the left rules
\Delta
contains at most one formula, with the extra condition for
(\rightarrow_{l})
that
\Delta
is not present in the left premise, and the right contraction rule
(\mathsf{c}_{r})
is omitted.

The cut rule. For single-conclusion calculi we require that
\Delta=\varnothing
and that
\Delta^{\prime}
contains at most one formula.
