\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbf{LK}}+(\mathit{GL})

Figure 6:
Modal sequent calculi.

Important to note is that the cut rule
is admissible in the presented calculi. It means that the rule can safely be added to the calculi without deriving new sequents.
Formally, a rule with premises
S_{1},\dots,S_{n}
and conclusion
S
is
admissible
in a sequent calculus
\mathbf{G}
iff for any substitution
\sigma
, if
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\sigma{S_{i}}
for all
i
, then
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\sigma{S}
.

Theorem 2

(Cut-admissibility)
.

Rule
(\mathsf{cut})
is admissible in all calculi from
\cref
fig:G3pc,fig:modal-sequent.

Cut-admissibility theorems feature prominently in proof theory. Indeed, the cut rule allows for easy completeness results, but the possibility to eliminate cut has far reaching applications such as consistency and decidability results. Cut-free calculi are used in Maehara’s proof-theoretic method for the CIP
[
60
]
. We will see in
\cref
sect:role_of_cut that some forms of cut can be adopted in Maehara’s method, but may create problems for the LIP.

Remark 3

(Design choices of sequent calculi)
.

Multiple choices can be made when designing a sequent calculus. Two common choices are: the type of sequents used (e.g., lists, multisets, or sets), and the choice between explicit or implicit structural rules. Generally, these choices make no difference for Maehara’s method to prove the CIP, only the cut rule may present a problem.
Pitts’ method for the UIP is more sensitive to such design choices where contraction rules create a problem (
\cref
subsec:Pitts).
\lipicsEnd

Remark 4

(Sequent calculi vs tableaux)
.

The intended reading of the sequent rules is from top to bottom: assuming the validity of the premises, the conclusion is valid. Dual to sequent calculi are tableau calculi
[
26
]
. These can be viewed as sequent calculi ‘upside down’, where the unsatisfiability of the upper formulas is preserved for the lower formulas. Maehara style methods also exist for tableau calculi
[
26
]
. See
Chapter 1
of this book for more details.
\lipicsEnd

3
Interpolation Using Sequent Calculi

In this section we discuss the basics of the proof-theoretic methods of interpolation properties based on propositional and modal logics.

3.1
Craig and Lyndon Interpolation via Maehara’s Method

There are different types of Craig interpolation depending on what consequence is under consideration (see other chapters in this book). Maehara’s method is very suitable for the
local
Craig interpolation property where we interpolate the local consequence relation represented by
\rightarrow
. It is also suitable for the local Lyndon interpolation property, which takes into account the polarity of propositional variables.

Definition 5

(Signature)
.

The
signature
of a formula
\varphi
, denoted
\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)
, is the set of propositional variables occurring in
\varphi
.

For the propositional language
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
and modal language
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
we inductively define the sets
\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\varphi)
of propositional variables occurring in
\varphi
with
positive
and
negative polarity
respectively:
\mathsf{Var^{+}}(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{p\}
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(p)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varnothing
for
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
;
\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\bot)=\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\top)=\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\top)=\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\bot)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\varnothing
; connectives
\vee
and
\wedge
preserve polarity; connective
\rightarrow
flips polarity in the antecedent and preserves polarity in the succedent; modality
\Box
preserves polarity.

For a multiset
\Gamma
, we define
\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigcup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)
, and analogously for
\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma)
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma)
. For a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
, we define
\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma)\cup\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Delta)
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma)\cup\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Delta)
, where polarity flips in the antecedent similarly to
\rightarrow
.

It is clear from the definition that
\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)=\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\varphi)\cup\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\varphi)
for any formula
\varphi
from
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
or
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
, and similarly for multisets and sequents.

Example 6
.

\mathsf{Var^{+}}((\Box p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow r)=\{p,r\}
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}((\Box p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow r)=\{q\}
.
Similarly,
\mathsf{Var^{+}}((\Box p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow q)=\{p,q\}
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}((\Box p\rightarrow q)\rightarrow q)=\{q\}
.
\lipicsEnd

Remark 7
.

We define the signature to consist of only propositional variables. One can also consider modalities as part of the signature. In the setting of first-order logic, the signature consists of relational and function symbols occurring in a first-order formula
\varphi
.
\lipicsEnd

Definition 8

(Craig interpolation)
.

A logic
L
over a language
\mathcal{L}
has the

Craig Interpolation Property (CIP)
, iff whenever
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
, there is a formula
\theta\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that:

1.

\mathsf{Var}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)\cap\mathsf{Var}(\psi)
,

2.

\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi\rightarrow\theta
and
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\theta\rightarrow\psi
.

Such formula
\theta
is called a
(Craig) interpolant
of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
in
L
.

Definition 9

(Lyndon interpolation)
.

The
Lyndon Interpolation Property (LIP)
for logics over language
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
or
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
is defined analogously to the CIP replacing condition
1
in
\cref
def:Craig-interpolation with the following condition:

[1’.]

1.

\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\varphi)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\psi)
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\varphi)\cap\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\psi)
.

In this case
\theta
is called a
Lyndon interpolant
of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
in
L
.

It is clear from the definition that the LIP implies the CIP.

Example 10

(Non-unique interpolants)
.

Given a provable implication
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
, there may exist multiple provably non-equivalent Craig/Lyndon interpolants. For example in
{\sf CPC}
, let
\varphi=(p\wedge q)\vee(\neg r\wedge s)
and
\psi=t\vee p\vee q\vee\neg r
. We have
\vdash_{\sf CPC}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. There are multiple provably non-equivalent interpolants for it, namely
(p\wedge q)\vee\neg r
,
p\vee\neg r
,
q\vee\neg r
, and
p\vee q\vee\neg r
.
\lipicsEnd

Now we introduce Maehara’s method and show how sequent calculi can be used to prove the CIP and LIP.
Maehara’s method
is commonly described as the proof-theoretic method of proving the CIP by an induction on a sequent proof (or proof in another suitable proof system). This is a helpful intuition. However, the method is more refined and interesting because of its more complex underlying process. The idea is to formulate a sequent-based interpolation property that is at least as strong as the CIP and that relies on a calculus with richer sequents called split sequents.

Definition 11

(Split sequent)
.

The expression of the form
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
is called a
split sequent
. Multisets
\Gamma
and
\Delta
belong to the
left split
, and
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime}
to the
right split
.

One can also think of sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
being the left split and sequent
\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
being the right split of split sequent
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
.

Definition 12

(Sequent Craig interpolation)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a sequent calculus over a language
\mathcal{L}
. We say that
\mathbf{G}
has the
Sequent Craig Interpolation Property (SCIP)
iff whenever we have
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
, there exists a formula
\theta\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that

1.

\mathsf{Var}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma,\Delta)\cap\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})
, and

2.

\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta
and
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\theta,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
.

If above properties hold we write
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
and we call
\theta
a
(Craig) interpolant
for split sequent
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{G}
. When dealing with a single-conclusion calculus we require
\Delta=\varnothing
and
\Delta^{\prime}
to contain at most one formula.

The intuition of the SCIP is that formula
\theta
interpolates between the left and right side of the split, rather than between the antecedent and succedent of the sequent. The splits are essential as explained after
\cref
thm:Craig-sequent->logic.

For sequent Lyndon interpolation we take into account the polarity of the variables:

Definition 13

(Sequent Lyndon interpolation)
.

The
Sequent Lyndon Interpolation Property (SLIP)
for logics over language
\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
or
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
is defined analogously to the SCIP replacing condition
1
in
\cref
def:Maehara with:

[1’.]

1.

\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\theta)\subseteq\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\cap\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})
.

In this case
\theta
is called a
Lyndon interpolant
for split sequent
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{G}
.

The interpolation property of a sequent calculus implies interpolation of its logic:

Theorem 14
.

If a sequent calculus
\mathbf{G}
has the SCIP/SLIP, then its logic
L
has the CIP/LIP.

Proof sketch.

Assume that
\mathbf{G}
has the SCIP/SLIP and let
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi
. By
\cref
CalculusForLogic,
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
. For any Craig/Lyndon interpolant
\theta
for the split
\varphi;\ \Rightarrow\ ;\psi
of this sequent in
\mathbf{G}
, one can prove that
\theta
is a Craig/Lyndon interpolant of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
in
L
.
∎

The idea of Maehara’s method is to present the sequent rules using split sequents, where each sequent rule has multiple counterparts in the split sequent calculus depending on whether the principal formula is on the left or on the right side of the split.
\Cref
fig:interpolation presents the split system of
{\mathbf{LK}}
. Given a split sequent proof of
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\varphi;\ \Rightarrow\ ;\psi
, one assigns Craig interpolants to the leaves and inductively defines the interpolants when going down the tree yielding an interpolant for
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\varphi;\ \Rightarrow\ ;\psi
. The splits ensure the variable property
1
of
\cref
def:Maehara. Indeed, bottom-up, the splits keep track of those formulas originating from
\varphi
on the left and those originating from
\psi
on the right side of the split. For example, in the following instance of rule
R(\rightarrow_{r})
, formula
\psi_{1}
should be stored on the right side of the split in the antecedent of the premise:

\varphi;\psi_{1}\Rightarrow\ ;\psi_{2}

\scriptstyle R(\rightarrow_{r})\;

\varphi;\Rightarrow\ ;\psi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{2}

In the split sequent calculus of
{\mathbf{LK}}
we see that active formulas are always on the same side as the principal formula, which is not necessarily the case for sequent calculi for other logics, see
\cref
remark:IPC.

Remark 15

(Monotone connectives)
.

When working with a language with only
monotone connectives
, Maehara’s split is redundant as the left and right sides can be taken as the left and right sides of the sequent arrow
\Rightarrow
(cf. symmetric sequents in
[
26
]
).

\lipicsEnd

Split axioms:

\scriptstyle\mathit{RR}(\mathsf{id})\;

;p\xRightarrow{\top}\ ;p

\scriptstyle\mathit{LR}(\mathsf{id})\;

p;\ \xRightarrow{p}\ ;p

\scriptstyle\mathit{RL}(\mathsf{id})\;

;p\xRightarrow{\neg p}p;

\scriptstyle\mathit{LL}(\mathsf{id})\;

p;\ \xRightarrow{\bot}p;

\scriptstyle L(\bot_{l})\;

\bot;\ \xRightarrow{\bot}\ ;

\scriptstyle R(\bot_{l})\;

;\bot\xRightarrow{\top}\ ;

\scriptstyle L(\top_{l})\;

;\ \xRightarrow{\bot}\top;

\scriptstyle R(\top_{r})\;

;\ \xRightarrow{\top}\ ;\top

Some unary split rules:

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi_{i}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle R(\wedge_{l}^{i})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi_{0}\wedge\varphi_{1}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\psi

\scriptstyle R(\rightarrow_{r})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta,\varphi,\varphi;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\mathsf{c}_{r})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta,\varphi;\Delta^{\prime}

Binary split rules:

\varphi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\vee_{l})\;

\varphi\vee\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\psi\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle R(\vee_{l})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\vee\psi\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\varphi,\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\psi,\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\wedge_{r})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}}\varphi\wedge\psi,\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\varphi

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\psi

\scriptstyle R(\wedge_{r})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\varphi\wedge\psi

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta,\varphi;\Delta^{\prime}

\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\rightarrow_{l})\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime},\varphi

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\psi\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle R(\rightarrow_{l})\;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\rightarrow\psi\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

Figure 7:
Craig interpolant construction with split sequent calculus for
{\mathbf{LK}}
. Capital letters
L
and
R
denote whether the principal formula is on the left or right side of the split respectively. The figure presents only three of the unary split rules, because all other unary split rules have the same shape in which the active formulas in the premise stay on the same side of the split as the principal formula of the conclusion and the interpolant of the conclusion is preserved from the premise.

We present Maehara’s method for classical propositional logic:

Theorem 16
.

{\mathbf{LK}}
has the SCIP and SLIP, and hence
{\sf CPC}
enjoys the CIP and LIP.

Proof.

Suppose
\vdash_{\mathbf{LK}}\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
and let
\pi
be its split sequent proof with rules from
\cref
fig:interpolation. We will construct an interpolant
\theta
by induction on the structure of
\pi
. For both the base step and the induction step, there are several subcases based on the lowest split sequent rule of
\pi
. The interpolant construction for each case is summarized in
\cref
fig:interpolation. Here we will check two cases. As most textbooks only consider Craig interpolants, here we show that this construction also satisfies the stronger variable condition for Lyndon interpolants.

•

\mathit{RR}(\mathsf{id}
): Suppose

{\small{\pi\ =\ {\vbox{\hbox{\kern 22.51755pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 16.97664pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$$}}}\kern 16.97664pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathit{RR}(\mathsf{id})\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to33.95328pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$;p\Rightarrow\ ;p$}\kern 0.0pt$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}}

We claim that
\theta=\top
. Indeed, the variable condition
1^{\prime}
from
\cref
def:sequent_Lyndon holds because
\mathsf{Var}(\top)=\varnothing
. Also,
\vdash_{\mathbf{LK}}\ \Rightarrow\top
by
(\top_{r})
and
\vdash_{\mathbf{LK}}\top,p\Rightarrow p
by
(\mathsf{id})
and
(\mathsf{w}_{l})
as desired.

•

L(\rightarrow_{l})
: Suppose that the bottommost rule is an instance of
(\rightarrow_{l})
with the principal formula on the left side of the split. For the premises the appropriate splits are chosen.

{\small{\pi\ =\ {{{{}{}{}}}{{{}{}{}}}\vbox{\hbox{\kern 19.85443pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.88948pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\ $}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\ \ $}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\ $}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.49988pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.6642pt\raise 10.10074pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle\pi_{1}}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 18.49988pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 18.49988pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 12.88948pt}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi;\Delta^{\prime}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.8867pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\ $}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\ \ $}}\kern 9.24994pt}\hbox{\hbox{$\ $}}}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{\hbox{$\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.49988pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}{}}{{}{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.6642pt\raise 10.10074pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.83124pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\scriptstyle\pi_{2}}$}}}}}}}}}}$}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 18.49988pt\hbox{\hbox{$$}}\kern 18.49988pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}\kern 12.8867pt}\kern 0.0pt\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$$}}\vbox{}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$$}\hss}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}$}}}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle L(\rightarrow_{l})\;$}}$}}\vbox{\vbox to0.4pt{\vfill\hbox to134.80185pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\vfill}}\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox{$\smash{\lower 2.31248pt\hbox{$$}}$}\hss}\kern 0.0pt}\kern 1.29166pt\hbox{\kern 28.1123pt\hbox{\hbox{$\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}$}}\kern 28.1123pt}}}\kern 0.0pt}}}}}

By the induction hypothesis (IH) on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, there are Lyndon interpolants
\theta_{1}
and
\theta_{2}
such that
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta,\varphi;\Delta^{\prime}
and
\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
. We prove that
\theta=\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}
is the desired interpolant. Out of the two variable conditions from
\cref
def:sequent_Lyndon, we show how to check the first (the other is symmetric):

\displaystyle\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2})=\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\theta_{1})\cup\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\theta_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\subseteq\Big(\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})\Big)\cup\Big(\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})\Big)\,(\text{IH})

\displaystyle\quad=\Big(\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi)\cup\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\Big)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad=\Big(\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\psi,\Gamma)\cup\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Delta,\varphi)\Big)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad=\Big(\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma)\cup\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\Delta)\Big)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad=\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\cap\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}).

It remains to check that
\vdash_{\mathbf{LK}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}
and
\vdash_{\mathbf{LK}}\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}
. Indeed:

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathit{IH}}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1},\varphi

\scriptstyle\mathsf{w}_{r}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\varphi

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathit{IH}}

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{2}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{w}_{r}\;

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{l}\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1},\theta_{2}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{c}_{r},\vee_{r}^{0},\vee_{r}^{1}\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathit{IH}}

\theta_{1},\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mathit{IH}}

\theta_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\vee_{l}\;

\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2},\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

We leave the other cases to the reader.
∎

Remark 17

(Computation and complexity)
.

Maehara’s method is
constructive
: it provides an algorithm to compute interpolants. The complexity of an interpolant of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is
linear
in the number of sequents occurring in the proof-tree of
\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
. Different proofs of a sequent may yield different interpolants.
\lipicsEnd

Remark 18

(Incompleteness)
.

Interestingly, it has been shown that Maehara’s method is
incomplete
in the sense that there are implications
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
for which there are interpolants that cannot be computed by Maehara’s method
[
38
]
(see also the next example).
\lipicsEnd

Example 19
.

Recall
\cref
ex:non-unique with
\varphi=(p\wedge q)\vee(\neg r\wedge s)
and
\psi=t\vee p\vee q\vee\neg r
. We work in the split calculus
{\mathbf{LK}}
(
\cref
fig:interpolation). Craig interpolant
p\vee\neg r
is derived as follows:

\scriptstyle\mathit{LR}(\mathsf{id})\;

p;\ \xRightarrow{p}\ ;p

\scriptstyle L(\mathsf{w}_{l}),R(\mathsf{w}_{r})\;

p,q;\ \xRightarrow{p}\ ;t,p,q,\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\wedge_{l})\;

p\wedge q;\ \xRightarrow{p}\ ;t,p,q,\neg r

\scriptstyle\mathit{RL}(\mathsf{id})\;

;r\xRightarrow{\neg r}r;

\scriptstyle R(\rightarrow_{r}),R(\mathsf{w}_{r})\;

\ \xRightarrow{\neg r}r;\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\bot_{l})\;

\bot;\ \xRightarrow{\bot}\ ;

\scriptstyle R(\mathsf{w}_{r})\;

\bot;\ \xRightarrow{\bot}\ ;\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\rightarrow_{l})\;

\neg r;\ \xRightarrow{\neg r\vee\bot(=\neg r)}\ ;\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\mathsf{w}_{l}),R(\mathsf{w}_{r})\;

\neg r,s;\ \xRightarrow{\neg r}\ ;t,p,q,\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\wedge_{l})\;

\neg r\wedge s;\ \xRightarrow{\neg r}\ ;t,p,q,\neg r

\scriptstyle L(\vee_{l}),R(\vee_{r})\;

(p\wedge q)\vee(\neg r\wedge s);\ \xRightarrow{p\vee\neg r}\ ;t\vee p\vee q\vee\neg r

A similar split sequent proof with the left branch starting with axiom
q;\xRightarrow{q};q
yields interpolant
q\vee\neg r
. Interpolant
(p\vee q)\vee\neg r
can be obtained by Maehara’s method using contraction rules. However, interpolant
(p\wedge q)\vee\neg r
cannot be obtained by Maehara’s method in
{\mathbf{LK}}
(see
[
38
]
).
\lipicsEnd

Remark 20

(Other types of sequent calculi)
.

Maehara’s method is very flexible and has been used for different types of sequent calculi (recall
\cref
remark:design_choices). We worked with two-sided (multiset) sequent calculus
{\mathbf{LK}}
. It equally works for sequents based on lists or sets, and when working with implicit weakening and contraction rules
[
81
]
. The method also works for
one-sided sequent
calculi based on formulas in negation normal form, where a one-sided split sequent has the form
\Delta_{l};\Delta_{r}
with left split
\Delta_{l}
and right split
\Delta_{r}
.
Recall from
\cref
remark:tableaux that sequent calculi can be viewed as the dual of tableau calculi. For that reason, Maehara’s method can be adjusted to tableaux (see
Chapter 1
.10 of this book).
\lipicsEnd

Remark 21

(Single-conclusion calculi)
.

Maehara’s method also applies to single-conclusion sequent calculi such as
{\mathbf{LJ}}
. The main difference is that sequents have only one formula in the succedent, so there is no need to split the succedent; equivalently, one may view the succedent as always belonging to the “right” side of the split. For
{\mathbf{LJ}}
, the construction of interpolants proceeds much as in the
{\mathbf{LK}}
case described in
\cref
fig:interpolation, except that rules such as
\mathit{RL}(\mathsf{id})
and
R(\wedge_{r})
are omitted, and the split rule
L(\rightarrow_{l})
is adapted as follows:

\Gamma^{\prime};\Gamma\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\varphi

\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\rightarrow_{l})\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\rightarrow\theta_{2}}\Delta^{\prime}

where the formula
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is on the left side of the split in the conclusion, but
\varphi
is forced to be on the ‘right’ side of the split in the left premise. This forces us to swap the sides of
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
\Gamma
in this premise, to make sure that
\Gamma
and
\varphi
remain on the same side of the split.
\lipicsEnd

In light of the previous remark we have the following theorem.

Theorem 22
.

Logic
{\sf IPC}
has the CIP and LIP.

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\varphi;

\scriptstyle L(\mathit{K})\;

\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\Diamond\theta}\Box\varphi;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta};\varphi

\scriptstyle R(\mathit{K})\;

\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\Box\theta};\Box\varphi

\varphi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\,;

\scriptstyle L(\mathit{D})\;

\Box\varphi,\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\Box\theta}\,;

\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime},\varphi\xRightarrow{\theta}\,;

\scriptstyle R(\mathit{D})\;

\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime},\Box\varphi\xRightarrow{\Box\theta}\,;

\Box\Gamma,\Gamma,\Box\varphi;\Box\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\varphi;

\scriptstyle L(\mathit{GL})\;

\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\Diamond\theta}\Box\varphi;

\Box\Gamma,\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\Box\varphi\xRightarrow{\theta};\varphi

\scriptstyle R(\mathit{GL})\;

\Box\Gamma;\Box\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\Box\theta};\Box\varphi

\varphi,\Box\varphi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\mathit{T})\;

\Box\varphi,\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta}\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}

Figure 8:
Interpolant transformations for modal rules. When the principal formula is on the left side of the split in rules
(\mathit{K})
and
(\mathit{GL})
, it is a ‘
\Diamond
-transformation’, and when it is on the right it is a ‘
\Box
-transformation’. It similarly works for rules
(\mathit{4})
and
(\mathit{S4})
from
\cref
fig:modal-rules. For rule
(\mathit{T})
, the same interpolant suffices for both splits. Both transformations for
(\mathit{D})
are ‘
\Box
-transformation’ because the only role
\varphi
plays there is to ensure that the antecedent is not empty. See
\cref
ex:unlabelled-modal-rules,Ex: box diamond.

For modal logics, we provide the interpolant transformations for the sequent calculi in
\cref
fig:modal-sequent, and show that logics
K
,
T
,
D
,
K4
,
S4
, and
GL
have the CIP. In fact, all of these except for
GL
admit the LIP. When considering split rules
L(\mathit{GL})
and
R(\mathit{GL})
from
\cref
fig:modal_Craig_transformations,
\varphi
and both
\Box\varphi
’s are placed on the same side of the split. Thus, the Craig condition in
\cref
def:Maehara(
1
) is satisfied, but the stronger Lyndon one in
\cref
def:sequent_Lyndon(
1^{\prime}
) cannot be guaranteed:
\Box\varphi
in the premise antecedent yields the opposite polarities from
\Box\varphi
in the conclusion succedent. Consequently, sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G.GL}}
can be used to prove the CIP but not the LIP, despite the fact that logic
GL
does have the LIP
[
79
]
. An existing proof-theoretic proof of the LIP for
GL

[
78
]
uses cyclic proofs, which are discussed in
Chapter 11
.
More generally, the existence of a cut-free sequent calculus is no guarantee that the logic has the CIP, nor the logic having the CIP is a guarantee that a cut-free sequent calculus can be used to prove it.

Although Maehara’s method is flexible across many types of logics, it does not mean that many logics of each type enjoy the CIP. In fact, interpolation is rare among, e.g., intermediate and modal logics
[
35
,
29
]
, which has crucial impact on proof theory (see
\cref
sect:results).

3.2
Restricted Cuts and Interpolation

In many textbooks on proof theory
[
81
,
80
]
, Maehara’s method is presented as an application of the cut-admissibility theorem. In the previous section we assumed to have a cut-free calculus, but it is well-known that special forms of cut can be admitted for Maehara’s method to prove the CIP (though not necessarily the LIP). This is especially useful for logics that do not admit a cut-free sequent calculus, but that have a complete calculus including restricted cut rules.

Restricted cut rules circumvent the problem of the general cut rule for Maehara’s method where one has no grip on the propositional variables occurring in the cut formula. We present three types of restricted cuts.

•

Analytic cut
: The
analytic cut rule

(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})
is presented in
\cref
fig:cutrules. The cut formula is restricted to subformulas of the conclusion sequent.
The rule preserves the
global subformula property
stating that in any proof each formula occurring in it is a subformula of the endsequent. Calculi with the subformula property are often called
analytic
, justifying the name of the rule. Examples of Maehara’s method with the analytic cut rule are for modal logic
S5

[
71
]
and bi-intuitionistic logic
[
48
]
.

•

Semi-analytic cut rules
:
semi-analyticity
is the concept in which the propositional variables of the cut formula occur in
one
formula from the conclusion. In other words, the cut formula is any (arbitrarily complex) combination of subformulas of a formula from the conclusion, justifying the name
semi-analytic
.
An example is
(\Box\mathsf{\text{-}semi\text{-}cut})
from
\cref
fig:cutrules introduced in
[
26
]
in a tableau calculus. In
\cref
sec:semi-analytic we discuss a slightly different notion of semi-analytic rules, which provides a general setting in which Maehara’s method can be applied.

•

Monochromatic cut
(e.g.,
[
17
]
): this type of cut is more general than semi-analytic cuts where the cut formula may be any combination of subformulas of
multiple
formulas in the conclusion. One has to be careful which
multiple
formulas are allowed and, therefore, this type of cut is only defined for split sequents: an application of the cut rule is
monochromatic
in split sequent proof
\pi
with endsequent
\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta_{1};\Delta_{2}
iff the cut formula
\varphi
satisfies the following condition:
\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma_{1})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\Delta_{1})
or
\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma_{2})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\Delta_{2})
, i.e., tracing the propositional variables only through the left or right side of Maehara’s split.

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\varphi,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\mathsf{an\text{-}cut}\;

\;\varphi\in\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\Box^{n}\psi

\Box^{n}\psi,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

\scriptstyle\Box\mathsf{\text{-}semi\text{-}cut}\;

\;\psi\in\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime},\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}

Figure 9:
Examples of restricted cut rules.

\Box\varphi,\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\mathit{T}\;

\Box\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\Box\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi,\Box\Delta

\scriptstyle\mathit{5}\;

\Sigma,\Box\Gamma\Rightarrow\Box\varphi,\Box\Delta

Figure 10:
Modal rules for
{\mathbf{G.S5}}
:
{\mathbf{G.S5}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbf{LK}}+(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})+(\mathit{T})+(\mathit{5})
.

To illustrate the use of such cuts in Maehara’s method, we consider modal logic
S5
as an example from
[
71
]
.
S5
is not known to have a cut-free sequent calculus. Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G.S5}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{\mathbf{LK}}+(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})+(\mathit{T})+(\mathit{5})
, including the analytic cut rule, is complete for
S5
(cf.
\cref
fig:G3pc,fig:cutrules,fig:GS5 for all rules).

Theorem 23
.

Modal logic
S5
has the CIP.

Proof.

The proof can be found in
[
71
]
and follows a Maehara-style argument such as in the proof of
\cref
thm:Maehara_CPC. We only state the interpolant constructions for the analytic cut rules and leave all other details to the reader. The interpolant construction for
(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})
depends on the formula in the conclusion whose subformula the cut formula
\varphi
is. There are two cases:

\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta_{1},\varphi;\Delta_{1}^{\prime}

\varphi,\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}

\scriptstyle L(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})\;

\;\varphi\in\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\vee\theta_{2}}\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}

\Gamma_{1};\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}}\Delta_{1};\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\varphi

\Gamma_{2};\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\varphi\xRightarrow{\theta_{2}}\Delta_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}

\scriptstyle R(\mathsf{an\text{-}cut})\;

\;\varphi\in\mathsf{Sub}(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime},\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime})

\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2};\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}\xRightarrow{\theta_{1}\wedge\theta_{2}}\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2};\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Delta_{2}^{\prime}

The variable condition
1
from
\cref
def:Maehara is fulfilled because of the subformula requirements.
∎

In fact,
S5
also admits the LIP. This cannot be shown using sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G.S5}}
, but can be obtained by using a hypersequent calculus for it (see
\cref
sec:hyper).

3.3
Uniform Interpolation via Pitts’ Method

The UIP is a beautiful strengthening of the CIP (see also
Chapter 9
, the main chapter on uniform interpolation). Here, we are interested in Pitts’
[
72
]
important result that
propositional quantification
can be interpreted in intuitionistic propositional logic
{\sf IPC}
(i.e., only using the propositional language). To show this, Pitts provides a proof-theoretic method showing that the UIP holds for
{\sf IPC}
. The method was later adapted to (intuitionistic) modal logics
[
11
,
41
,
5
]
. The purpose of this section is to explain the main concepts in Pitts’ method.

A Craig interpolant of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is
uniform
if it
only
relies on
\varphi
(or only on
\psi
) meaning that it is a Craig interpolant that uniformly works for any provable implication
\varphi\rightarrow\psi^{\prime}
with
\psi^{\prime}
in a specified vocabulary.
Formally, we define the following, where a formula
\varphi
is
p
-free
iff
p\notin\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)
,
p^{+}
-free
iff
p\notin\mathsf{Var^{+}}(\varphi)
, and
p^{-}
-free
iff
p\notin\mathsf{Var^{-}}(\varphi)
:

Definition 24

(Uniform interpolation)
.

Fix a logic
L
over a language
\mathcal{L}
. We say that
L
has the

Uniform Interpolation Property (UIP)
iff for any formula
\varphi\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
and propositional variable
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
, there are formulas
\exists p\varphi,\forall p\varphi
over the same language
\mathcal{L}
such that the following hold:

1.

\exists p\varphi
and
\forall p\varphi
are
p
-free, and
\mathsf{Var}(\exists p\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)
and
\mathsf{Var}(\forall p\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\varphi)
;

2.

\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi\rightarrow\exists p\varphi
and
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\forall p\varphi\rightarrow\varphi
;

3.

For all
p
-free formulas
\psi
:

\displaystyle\text{if }\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\text{ then }\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\exists p\varphi\rightarrow\psi.

\displaystyle\text{if }\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\psi\rightarrow\varphi,\text{ then }\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\psi\rightarrow\forall p\varphi.

Formula
\exists p\varphi
is called the
uniform post-interpolant
of
\varphi
w.r.t.
p
, and
\forall p\varphi
is the
uniform pre-interpolant
of
\varphi
w.r.t.
p
in
L
. When
\exists p\varphi
(resp.
\forall p\varphi
) exists for all formulas
\varphi\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
, then we say that
L
has the
Post-Uniform Interpolation Property (PostUIP)
(resp.
Pre-Uniform Interpolation Property (PreUIP)
).

The notations
\exists p\varphi
and
\forall p\varphi
are suggestive: the formulas do not really contain quantifiers, but they are defined over the (propositional/modal) language
\mathcal{L}
. However, the notation is justified by Pitts’ result stating that uniform interpolants
interpret propositional quantifiers
of second order intuitionistic logic into the propositional language.
Uniform interpolants are also connected to so-called
bisimulation quantifiers
(see
Chapter 9
of this book). In the literature, and particularly in
Chapter 1
, post-uniform and pre-uniform interpolants are known as right-uniform and left-uniform interpolants respectively.

Remark 25
.

In classical logic, we can define
\exists p\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\neg\forall p(\neg\varphi)
.
\lipicsEnd

The proof of the following property can be found, e.g., in
[
87
]
.

Theorem 26

(Uniqueness)
.

Let
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
both be uniform pre-interpolants for
\varphi
w.r.t.
p
in logic
L
. Then
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\chi_{1}\rightarrow\chi_{2}
and
\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}}\chi_{2}\rightarrow\chi_{1}
. The same holds for uniform post-interpolants.

Recently, Kurahashi
[
50
]
introduced the notion of uniform Lyndon interpolation:

Definition 27

(Uniform Lyndon interpolation)
.

A logic
L
over a language
\mathcal{L}
has the
Uniform Lyndon Interpolation Property (ULIP)
, iff for any
\varphi\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
and
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
, there are formulas
\forall^{+}\!p\,\varphi
,
\forall^{-}\!p\,\varphi
,
\exists^{+}\!p\,\varphi
, and
\exists^{-}\!p\,\varphi
in
{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
that satisfy the properties from
\cref{def:uniforminterpolation}
, except that each occurrence of
p
-free is replaced with
p^{+}
-free for
\forall^{+}\!p\,\varphi
and
\exists^{+}\!p\,\varphi
and with
p^{-}
-free for
\forall^{-}\!p\,\varphi
and
\exists^{-}\!p\,\varphi
.

Theorem 28

(
[
50
]
)
.

If a logic
L
has the UIP/ULIP, then it has the CIP/LIP.

In fact, uniform interpolants are the
weakest
and
strongest
Craig interpolants.

Now we turn to the proof-theoretic method by Pitts.
The ingredients for Pitts’ method in comparison to Maehara’s method are summarized in
\cref
table:overview.

Maehara’s method

Pitts’ method

Craig interpolation

Uniform interpolation

Sequent formulation of the Craig IP

Sequent formulation of the uniform IP

‘Cut-free’ sequent calculus

‘Strongly terminating’ sequent calculus

Proof of
\varphi\rightarrow\psi

Proof search of
\varphi

Table 1:
General overview of similarities and differences between Maehara’s and Pitts’ method. The term ‘cut-free’ is in quotes, because cut-free systems are nice for Maehara’s method, but certainly not necessary (recall
\cref
sect:role_of_cut). Similarly, for Pitts’ method, strongly terminating calculi are a nice feature to prove the UIP, but not necessary (see
\Cref
remark:terminating).

Because of technical reasons we will not prove the UIP for
{\sf IPC}
, as in the original proof by Pitts
[
72
]
, but we will explain Pitts’ method for modal logic
K
as done by Bílková in
[
11
]
. Recall
\cref
remark:classical_dual_ui: we only focus on the construction of pre-interpolants
\forall p\varphi
.

Towards a sequent based formulation of the UIP we have the following in mind. A pre-interpolant for
\varphi
w.r.t.
p
works almost as a Craig interpolant for all implications
\psi\rightarrow\varphi
where
\psi
is
p
-free (almost, because it might violate the Craig variable condition). So
\varphi
on the right is fixed, but
\psi
on the left is flexible. This idea will be present in the split sequents in the following definition, where
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
is fixed on the right of the split, and the
p
-free contexts
\Pi
and
\Sigma
are flexible and always on the left side of the split.

Definition 29
.

A sequent calculus
\mathbf{G}
over a language
\mathcal{L}
has the
Sequent Pre-Uniform Interpolation Property (SPreUIP)
iff for any sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
and
p\in\mathsf{Prop}
, there exists a formula
A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\in{\sf Form}_{\mathcal{L}}
such that the following properties hold:

1.

\mathsf{Var}(A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta))\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\Gamma,\Delta)\setminus\{p\}
;

2.

\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Gamma,A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\Rightarrow\Delta
;

3.

\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Pi\Rightarrow A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta),\Sigma
for any
p
-free multisets
\Pi
and
\Sigma
such that
\vdash_{\mathbf{G}}\Pi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Sigma,\Delta
.

Remark 30
.

We only focus on modal logic
K
for which above sequent formulation of the UIP is sufficient because of
\cref
remark:classical_dual_ui. When considering intuitionistic logics one additionally has to define a formula
E_{p}(\Gamma)
that satisfies dual-like properties (not exactly dual since the construction of
E_{p}(\Gamma)
depends on
A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi)
). Here we leave out all such details and refer the reader to
[
72
,
5
]
.
\lipicsEnd

Remark 31
.

Here we only define a sequent version for the UIP. One can also provide a sequent version for the ULIP, see
[
4
]
.
\lipicsEnd

Theorem 32
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a sequent calculus for a logic
L
. If
\mathbf{G}
has the SPreUIP, then
L
has the PreUIP.

Proof.

Define
\forall p\varphi\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}A_{p}(\ \Rightarrow\varphi)
. We leave it to the reader to check the details.
∎

A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)
is constructed using the sequent calculus. Whereas for Maehara’s method we are given a
provable
split sequent
\Gamma;\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta;\Delta^{\prime}
, here we are given a sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
that might be
unprovable
. So we cannot look at a
proof
of
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
because there simply might not be one. The trick is to construct the uniform interpolant via a
proof search
of
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
. A proof search of a sequent is a tree in which, bottom-up, rules of the sequent calculus are applied in an attempt to find a proof. A key feature that we want of this proof search is that it is
finite
. This helps ensure that the algorithm constructing the uniform pre-interpolant terminates.

Definition 33

(Strong termination)
.

A sequent calculus is said to be
strongly terminating
iff for each sequent, any order of bottom-up applications of the rules results in a finite tree, i.e., it stops with leaves that are either axioms or unprovable sequents to which no rule can be applied.

Remark 34
.

We focus on strongly terminating calculi, but weaker forms of termination may also work, though they typically require additional machinery, e.g., for modal logic
T

[
11
]
, for a hypersequent calculus to prove the UIP for
S5

[
86
]
, or in cyclic proofs
[
1
]
.
\lipicsEnd

Many sequent calculi are not strongly terminating. For example, sequent calculus
{\mathbf{LJ}}
is not strongly terminating due to contraction rule
(\mathsf{c}_{l})
(see
\cref
fig:interpolation), but even when the explicit weakening and contraction rules are subsumed as in
{\mathbf{G3ip}}
from
[
81
]
, strong termination still fails, due to the intuitionistic version of rule
(\rightarrow_{l})
where contraction is embedded as follows:

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle(\rightarrow_{l})^{*}\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

where
\Delta
has at most one formula. Consequently, Pitts used a strongly terminating calculus
{\mathbf{G4ip}}
for
{\sf IPC}
where
(\rightarrow_{l})^{*}
is replaced with four other implication rules
[
22
,
39
,
89
,
90
]
, an idea later extended to intuitionistic modal logics
[
43
]
.

We look at modal logic
K
. Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G.K}}
is not strongly terminating because of the contraction rules. Instead, we will consider calculus
{\mathbf{G3K}}
from
\cref
fig:G3K with implicit weakening and contraction.

\scriptstyle(\mathsf{id})^{*}\;

p,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,p

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi

\scriptstyle(\mathit{K})^{*}\;

\Pi,\Box\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\Box\varphi

\scriptstyle(\bot_{l})^{*}\;

\bot,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{l}\;

\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\vee_{l}\;

\varphi\vee\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\varphi,\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle\wedge_{l}\;

\varphi\wedge\psi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta

\scriptstyle(\top_{r})^{*}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\top

\varphi,\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\psi

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{r}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi\rightarrow\psi

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi,\psi

\scriptstyle\vee_{r}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi\vee\psi

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\psi

\scriptstyle\wedge_{r}\;

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi\wedge\psi

Figure 11:
Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G3K}}
.

Theorem 35
.

Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G3K}}
for
K
is strongly terminating.

Proof.

We define the weight
w(\varphi)
of a formula
\varphi
to be the number of symbols occurring in it counted as a multiset. For sequents, we define
w(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\Sigma_{\varphi\in\Gamma}w(\varphi)
. For all rules in
{\mathbf{G3K}}
it holds that the weight of each premise is smaller than the weight of the conclusion, meaning that the calculus is strongly terminating.
∎

Remark 36
.

Modal sequent rules
(\mathit{4})
and
(\mathit{T})
from
\cref
fig:modal-rules prevent strong termination, because rule
(\mathit{4})
duplicates the context
\Gamma
and rule
(\mathit{T})
duplicates principal formula
\varphi
in the premise. In fact, logics
K4
and
S4
do not have the UIP
[
11
,
34
]
.

\lipicsEnd

Theorem 37
.

Sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G3K}}
has the SPreUIP, and hence logic
K
enjoys the UIP.

Proof idea.

By recursion on
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
we construct the formula
A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)
and prove that it acts as the pre-interpolant of
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
w.r.t.
p
.
The algorithm starts a proof search bottom-up for sequent
\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta
and inductively defines the uniform interpolants. The algorithm follows the steps from
\cref
fig:uip according to the shape of the sequents with priority given to the higher rows in the table. The left column of the table indicates the rule applied in the proof search, the middle column the shape of the conclusion of that rule, and the right column defines the pre-interpolant using the premise(s) of that rule. Rows 2–7 correspond to so-called
invertible rules
. We stress that for each invertible rule, the pre-interpolant for the conclusion can always be taken to be a conjunction of the pre-interpolants of the premises. The last row is a special case corresponding to the
non-invertible
rule
(\mathit{K})
providing the
\Box
-clauses in the result, but it also corresponds to the possibility in which the proof search ‘applies rule
(\mathit{K})
’ on a boxed formula of the extra context
\Sigma
on the ‘left split’ from condition
3
of
\cref
def:seq_uip providing the
\Diamond
-clause in the result. The algorithm terminates in the same ordering on weights
w
in which sequent calculus
{\mathbf{G3K}}
terminates (
\cref
thm:termination). Indeed, all rows except for the latter reflect the rules of the calculus, and also the recursion in the last row reduces in the same way.

rule

\displaystyle\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta\textit{ matches}

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta)\textit{ equals}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id})^{*}

\displaystyle r,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},r

\displaystyle\top

\displaystyle(\top_{r})^{*}

\displaystyle\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\top

\displaystyle\top

\displaystyle(\bot_{l})^{*}

\displaystyle\bot,\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta

\displaystyle\top

\displaystyle(\wedge_{l})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime},\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma^{\prime},\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta)

\displaystyle(\vee_{l})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime},\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma^{\prime},\psi_{1}\Rightarrow\Delta)\wedge A_{p}(\Gamma,\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta)

\displaystyle(\rightarrow_{l})

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime},\psi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\Delta,\psi_{1})\wedge A_{p}(\Gamma,\psi_{2}\Rightarrow\Delta)

\displaystyle(\wedge_{r})

\displaystyle\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{1})\wedge A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{2})

\displaystyle(\vee_{r})

\displaystyle\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{1},\psi_{2})

\displaystyle(\rightarrow_{r})

\displaystyle\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{1}\rightarrow\psi_{2}

\displaystyle A_{p}(\Gamma,\psi_{1}\Rightarrow\Delta^{\prime},\psi_{2})

\displaystyle(\mathit{K})^{*}\text{ or no}

\displaystyle\Box\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi\Rightarrow\Box\Delta^{\prime},\Psi\

\displaystyle\Diamond A_{p}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\varnothing)\vee\bigvee_{q\in\Phi\setminus\{p\}}q\vee\bigvee_{r\in\Psi\setminus\{p\}}\neg r\vee\bigvee_{\psi\in\Delta^{\prime}}\Box A_{p}(\Gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow\psi)

rule applies

\displaystyle\text{where }\Gamma^{\prime}\neq\varnothing

\displaystyle(\mathit{K})^{*}\text{ or no}

\displaystyle\Phi\Rightarrow\Box\Delta^{\prime},\Psi\

\displaystyle\bigvee_{q\in\Phi\setminus\{p\}}q\vee\bigvee_{r\in\Psi\setminus\{p\}}\neg r\vee\bigvee_{\psi\in\Delta^{\prime}}\Box A_{p}(\Rightarrow\psi)

rule applies

Table 2:
Uniform interpolant construction for
{\mathbf{G3K}}
, where
\Phi
and
\Psi
do not contain boxed formulas.

It remains to check the correctness of this algorithm, i.e., we need to check the conditions of
\cref
def:seq_uip. This is based on syntactic arguments for which we refer to
[
11
]
.
∎

Example 38
.

Let us apply the uniform interpolant algorithm for
{\mathbf{G3K}}
to formula
\Box p\vee\Box\neg p
. We present it in a proof search where we compute
A_{p}(\cdot)
of each sequent denoted in parentheses:

\ \Rightarrow p\ \ (\bot)

p\Rightarrow\bot\ \ (\bot)

\scriptstyle\rightarrow_{r}\;

\ \Rightarrow\neg p\ \ (\bot)

.

Proof search on rule (
\mathit{K}
)

\ \Rightarrow\Box p,\Box\neg p\ \ (\Box\bot\vee\Box\bot)

\scriptstyle\vee_{r}\;

\ \Rightarrow\Box p\vee\Box\neg p\ \ (\Box\bot\vee\Box\bot)

For the top sequents, no rule of
{\mathbf{G3K}}
applies, and the last row of
\cref
fig:uip applies with the empty disjunction yielding
\bot
. We leave it to the reader to check the conditions of
\cref
def:seq_uip.
\lipicsEnd

Similar proof-theoretic proofs of uniform interpolation have been provided for classical modal logics
GL
,
S4.Grz
,
T

[
11
]
, and
D

[
41
]
, as well as for intuitionistic modal logics
iK
,
iKD

[
42
]
, and
iSL

[
23
]
. The method has been extended to general forms of calculi providing results for a wide range of intermediate and substructural logics as discussed in the next section.

The uniform interpolation algorithm has been formalized in the interactive theorem prover Coq/Rocq for logics
{\sf IPC}
,
K
,
GL
, and
iSL

[
24
,
23
]
. This has resulted in an online calculator of uniform interpolants
[
24
,
23
]
. This calculator has been useful to prove that certain second-order intuitionistic connectives are non-definable
[
46
]
.

4
Interpolation in Universal Proof Theory

Universal proof theory
, initiated by Iemhoff
[
42
,
41
]
and extended in later works
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
7
,
6
]
, investigates general questions about the existence, equivalence, and characterization of well-behaved proof systems across broad classes of logics. It offers a uniform and modular method to establish logical properties, such as the CIP and UIP and the admissibility of Visser rules
[
6
]
, by linking them to the existence of certain structurally constrained sequent calculi. This framework applies to many logics, notably intuitionistic, modal, and substructural ones, by defining syntactic conditions that guarantee these properties.

The method yields both positive and negative results. Positively, if a logic admits a (terminating)
semi-analytic
calculus satisfying the required structural conditions, it enjoys the CIP (and UIP). In
\cref
sec:semi-analytic we introduce these calculi and see that they provide a general form for axioms and rules, such that as a result, the framework provides a systematic way to prove interpolation properties. Negatively, and more significantly, since these properties are rare, these results indicate that most logics cannot have a semi-analytic
(resp. terminating semi-analytic) calculus which we will discuss in
\cref
sect:results. In this sense, it not only provides a systematic way to prove interpolation results but also demonstrates the non-existence of well-behaved calculi for large classes of logics.

Although interpolation properties were the first to be investigated within this line of research,
[
6
]
introduces a new logical invariant: the admissibility of the Visser rules. This extension significantly broadens the scope of universal proof theory, moving beyond interpolation to encompass further proof-theoretic properties. Such a generalization is particularly valuable for obtaining negative results, as it provides new tools to capture the limitations of proof systems. In
[
6
]
, a constructive sequent calculus is introduced, and one interesting result is that no intermediate logic other than
{\sf IPC}
admits a constructive sequent calculus.

4.1
Semi-analytic Rules and Interpolation

The following definition introduces the so-called
semi-analytic
rules, a concept that is slightly weaker than the analyticity property of rules: a rule is
analytic
when the formulas in the premises are subformulas of the principal formula in the conclusion. As a result, these rules are broader and more general than the analytic ones.

Definition 39

(Semi-analytic rules,
[
5
,
7
]
)
.

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
, let
\Gamma_{i}
’s,
\Delta_{i}
’s, and
\Pi_{j}
’s be pairwise disjoint families of pairwise distinct multiset variables,
\overline{\varphi}_{ir}
’s,
\overline{\psi}_{js}
’s, and
\overline{\chi}_{js}
’s be multisets of formulas and
\varphi
a formula, where
1\leq r\leq k_{i}
and
1\leq s\leq l_{j}
.

A rule is called
left single-conclusion semi-analytic
if it has the form

\{\Pi_{j},\overline{\psi}_{js}\Rightarrow\overline{\chi}_{js}\mid 1\leq j\leq m,1\leq s\leq l_{j}\}

\quad\{\Gamma_{i},\overline{\varphi}_{ir}\Rightarrow\Delta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq r\leq k_{i}\}

\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{m},\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n},\varphi\Rightarrow\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{n}

and it satisfies the following variable condition:

\bigcup_{ir}\mathsf{Var}(\overline{\varphi}_{ir})\cup\bigcup_{js}\mathsf{Var}(\overline{\psi}_{js})\cup\bigcup_{js}\mathsf{Var}(\overline{\chi}_{js})\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\varphi).

In each instance of the rule, at most one of
\Delta_{i}
’s can be substituted by a formula and the rest must be empty, as the rule is single-conclusion.

A rule is called
right single-conclusion semi-analytic
if it has the form

\{\Gamma_{i},\overline{\varphi}_{ir}\Rightarrow\overline{\chi}_{ir}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq r\leq k_{i}\}

\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}\Rightarrow\varphi

and the following variable condition holds:

\bigcup_{ir}\mathsf{Var}(\overline{\varphi}_{ir})\cup\bigcup_{ir}\mathsf{Var}(\overline{\chi}_{ir})\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\varphi).

A
multi-conclusion semi-analytic
rule can be defined similarly, when we allow the rule to be multi-conclusion (see
[
7
, Definition 4.3]
). A rule is called
semi-analytic
if it is a single-conclusion or multi-conclusion left or right semi-analytic rule.

Example 40
.

A generic example of a left single-conclusion semi-analytic rule is the following:

\Pi,\nu^{1}_{11},\nu^{2}_{11}\Rightarrow\rho_{11}

\Pi,\nu_{12}\Rightarrow\rho_{12}

\Gamma,\mu^{1}_{1},\mu^{2}_{1},\mu^{3}_{1}\Rightarrow\Delta

\Pi,\Gamma,\mu\Rightarrow\Delta

where

\mathsf{Var}(\mu^{1}_{1})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\mu^{2}_{1})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\mu_{1}^{3})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\nu_{11}^{1})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\nu_{11}^{2})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\nu_{12})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\rho_{11})\cup\mathsf{Var}(\rho_{12})\subseteq\mathsf{Var}(\mu)
.

The left and middle premises are in the same family with the context
\Pi
in the antecedent. Thus, one copy of
\Pi
appears in the antecedent of the conclusion.
\lipicsEnd

Example 41
.

Concrete examples of multi-conclusion semi-analytic rules are the rules in
\cref
fig:G3pc, except for the cut rule. The single-conclusion version of these rules are single-conclusion semi-analytic rules.
Concrete non-examples of multi-conclusion semi-analytic rules include the cut rule, as it violates the variable condition. Considering the modal rules in
\cref
fig:modal-rules, rules
(\mathit{T})
and
(\mathit{S4})
are single-conclusion semi-analytic,
(\mathit{K})
and
(\mathit{D})
are non-examples as the contexts do not remain intact from the premises to the conclusion, and
(\mathit{4})
and
(\mathit{GL})
are also non-examples, as context
\Gamma
disappears from the premise to the conclusion.
Non-examples for single-conclusion semi-analytic rules are provided by the single-conclusion version of the mentioned rules, i.e.,
(\mathit{K})
,
(\mathit{D})
,
(\mathit{4})
,
(\mathit{GL})
, and
(\mathsf{cut})
. For another non-example, consider the following rule in the calculus
\mathbf{KC}
introduced in
[
15
]
for the logic
\mathsf{KC}={\sf IPC}+\neg\varphi\vee\neg\neg\varphi
:

\Gamma,\varphi\Rightarrow\psi,\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Delta

\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\varphi

\ \ \psi,\Pi\Rightarrow\Lambda

\Gamma,\varphi\rightarrow\psi,\Pi\Rightarrow\Delta,\Lambda

in which
\Delta
must be substituted by multisets of
strictly negative formulas
(not containing all formulas in the language; see
[
15
]
).
This rule is not semi-analytic as its context
\Delta
is not free for arbitrary substitutions of multisets.
\lipicsEnd

Remark 42
.

Note that the semi-analytic cut rules introduced in
\cref
subsec:Maehara do not fall in the category of semi-analytic rules as per
\cref
semi-analyticRules. The reason is the simple fact that in a cut rule, there is no principal formula. However, it is possible to generalize the notion to also cover semi-analytic cut rules, a direction we leave for future work.

\lipicsEnd

Now, we define a general form for axioms.

Definition 43

(Focused axioms,
[
7
]
)
.

A meta-sequent is a
focused axiom
, if it has one of the forms:

\varphi\Rightarrow\varphi\quad\quad\quad\quad\Rightarrow\bar{\psi}\quad\quad\quad\quad\bar{\theta}\Rightarrow\quad\quad\quad\quad\Gamma,\bar{\varphi}\Rightarrow\Delta\quad\quad\quad\quad\Gamma\Rightarrow\bar{\varphi},\Delta

where for each axiom
\mathsf{Var}(\mu)=\mathsf{Var}(\nu)
, for any
\mu,\nu\in\bar{\eta}
and
\eta\in\{\varphi,\psi,\theta\}
.

Note that the term
focused
is different from the notion of ‘focused proofs’ used in proof-search, as in the works
[
64
,
58
]
.

Example 44
.

The axioms in sequent calculi
{\mathbf{LK}}
and
{\mathbf{LJ}}
are all focused. More examples:

p,\neg p\Rightarrow\quad\quad\quad\quad\Rightarrow p,\neg p\quad\quad\quad\quad\Gamma,\neg\top\Rightarrow\Delta\quad\quad\quad\quad\Gamma\Rightarrow\Delta,\neg\bot

For a non-example, consider the axiom
(p,\neg p,q\Rightarrow\,)
, where
p
and
q
are distinct atoms. This axiom is not focused, as the set of the variables of
p
and
q

are not equal.
\lipicsEnd

Definition 45
.

A calculus
\mathbf{G}
is
(single-conclusion) multi-conclusion semi-analytic
if each rule in
\mathbf{G}
is (single-conclusion) multi-conclusion semi-analytic and its axioms are (single-conclusion versions of) focused axioms. In case the language contains the modality
\Box
, any union of the sets of rules
\{(\mathit{K})\},\{(\mathit{4})\},\{(\mathit{K}),(\mathit{D})\},\{(\mathit{K}),(\mathit{T})\}
are also allowed to appear in
\mathbf{G}
.

We finish this subsection by defining what it means for a calculus to be fully terminating. This is a stronger notion than
strong termination
introduced in
\cref
def:termination.

Definition 46

(Fully terminating sequent calculi)
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be a calculus and
\preceq
be a well-founded order on sequents. We say that
\mathbf{G}
is
fully terminating with respect to
\preceq

if for any sequent
S
there are at most finitely many instances of the rules in
\mathbf{G}
with the conclusion
S
. Moreover, we require the following to be strictly less than
S
with respect to the order
\preceq
:

•

the premises of all instances of a rule where the conclusion is
S
,

•

proper subsequents of
S
, and

•

in case that the language contains the modality
\Box
, any
S^{\prime}=(\Gamma,\Pi\Rightarrow\Delta,\Lambda)
, where
\Pi\cup\Lambda\neq\varnothing
and
S=(\Gamma,\Box\Pi\Rightarrow\Delta,\Box\Lambda)
.

The calculus
\mathbf{G}
is
fully terminating
if there is an order
\preceq
such that
\mathbf{G}
is fully terminating with respect to
\preceq
.

Remark 47
.

In modal logics, a fully terminating semi-analytic calculus cannot contain the modal rules
(\mathit{4})
and
(\mathit{T})
presented in Figure
6
. This means that any fully semi-analytic modal sequent calculus can contain the set of modal rules
\{(\mathit{K})\}
and
\{(\mathit{K}),(\mathit{D})\}
.
\lipicsEnd

A useful rule of thumb for interpolation is that if a logic
L
has a semi-analytic calculus
\mathbf{G}
, then
L
enjoys the CIP, and if
\mathbf{G}
is fully terminating, it also enjoys the UIP. The proofs essentially follow Maehara’s approach for the CIP and Pitts’ method for the UIP. Of course, there are a lot of technical details to check for which we refer to
[
7
,
5
]
.

4.2
Interpolation and (Non-)Existence of Sequent Calculi

In this section, we apply the uniform methodology introduced in the context of universal proof theory and present a rich number of positive and negative results based on the CIP and UIP across different logics.
For a positive result we first observe that a given logic has a (fully terminating) semi-analytic calculus, which allows us to conclude that this logic has the CIP (UIP). For a negative result we use the contraposition: if we know that a logic does not have the CIP (UIP), we immediately conclude that this logic cannot have a (fully terminating) semi-analytic calculus.

4.2.1
Intermediate and Modal Logics

The following theorem is the main positive result with regard to proving the CIP and UIP.

Theorem 48

(
[
7
,
5
]
)
.

Let
L
be a logic over a language
\mathcal{L}\in\{\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}},\mathcal{L}_{\Box}\}
and
\mathbf{G}
be a single-conclusion (resp. multi-conclusion) semi-analytic calculus for
L
.

•

If
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\sf{p}}
and if
{\sf IPC}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}
(resp.
\textnormal{{L}}={\sf CPC}
), then
L
has the CIP. Moreover, if
\mathbf{G}
is fully terminating, then
L
has the UIP.

•

If
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
and if
\textnormal{{\sf K}}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}
, then
L
has the CIP. Moreover, if
\mathbf{G}
is fully terminating, then
L
has the UIP.

The theorem in its current form does not yield strong general positive results for two reasons. First, in the realm of intermediate logic it is known that only seven consistent intermediate logics have the CIP and UIP, namely
{\sf IPC}
,
\mathsf{LC}
,
\mathsf{KC}
,
\mathsf{Bd_{2}}
,
\mathsf{Sm}
,
\mathsf{GSc}
, and
{\sf CPC}

[
61
]
. Second, for modal calculi, being semi-analytic depends on an ad-hoc examination of rules, as we currently lack a general form for a semi-analytic modal rule.
However, for modal logics there are many proof-theoretic proofs of the CIP, e.g., for modal logic
GL

[
11
]
and intuitionistic modal logics in
[
42
]
(see also
\Cref
sec:basics).

To state our negative results (the following corollaries), we rely on the nonexistence of the CIP and UIP in a wide range of logics
[
12
,
34
,
35
,
61
,
62
]
. Consequently, we get the nonexistence of (fully terminating) calculi consisting only of semi-analytic rules and focused axioms.

Corollary 49

(Negative results, CIP)
.

The following results hold:

•

Except for logics
{\sf IPC}
,
\mathsf{LC}
,
\mathsf{KC}
,
\mathsf{Bd_{2}}
,
\mathsf{Sm}
,
\mathsf{GSc}
, and
{\sf CPC}
, no intermediate logic has a single- or multi-conclusion semi-analytic sequent calculus.

•

With the possible exception of at most 37 for
S4
and 6 for
\mathsf{Grz}
, none of their consistent extensions have a single- or multi-conclusion semi-analytic sequent calculus.

Corollary 50

(Negative results, UIP)
.

With the possible exception of at most six extensions of
S4
, none of the extensions of either
S4
or
K4
have a single- or multi-conclusion fully terminating semi-analytic sequent calculus.

4.2.2
(Modal) Substructural and Linear Logics

The results of the previous section are generalized to (modal) substructural and linear logics. Consider the
bounded
language
\mathcal{L}^{b}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\wedge,\vee,\to,*,0,1,\top,\bot\}
and the
unbounded
language
\mathcal{L}^{u}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{L}^{b}\setminus\{\top,\bot\}
. Denote
\mathcal{L}_{!}^{b}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{L}^{b}\cup\{!\}
and
\mathcal{L}_{!}^{u}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathcal{L}^{u}\cup\{!\}
.

, and
{\mathbf{RIMALL}}
respectively.

\cref

in the language by

to mean
\mathbf{FL}_{\mathbf{e}}+\{\mathit{K}!\}
, write

to mean

, or write
\mathbf{FL}_{\mathbf{e}}\mathbf{T}
to mean
\mathbf{FL}_{\mathbf{e}}+\{!_{l}\}
, and likewise, we write
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\sf{K}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{ew}}\sf{D}
, and
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\sf{T}
for their corresponding logics. The following calculi are proved to enjoy cut admissibility in

Theorem 52

(
[
7
]
)
.

Let
L
be a logic over
\mathcal{L}\in\{\mathcal{L}^{b},\mathcal{L}^{u},\mathcal{L}_{!}^{b},\mathcal{L}_{!}^{u}\}
and
\mathbf{G}
be a single-conclusion (resp. multi-conclusion) semi-analytic calculus for
L
. Then,

•

if
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{b}
or
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{!}^{b}
and

(resp.
{\mathsf{MALL}}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}

L
has the CIP, and

•

if
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{u}
or
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{!}^{u}
and
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}
(resp.
\mathsf{CFL}_{\mathsf{e}}\subseteq\textnormal{{L}}
), then
L
has the CIP.

Corollary 53

(Positive results)
.

Concrete examples of the logics that enjoy the CIP are the logics of the sequent calculi in
\cref
thm: cut mardom.

Proof.

The presented sequent calculi for these logics consist of focused axioms and semi-analytic rules. Therefore, by
\cref
MainCor, we get the CIP for their logics.
∎

We use a series of facts regarding the nonexistence of the CIP in a wide range of logics to obtain our negative results. Before that, we recall some of these logics.
\Cref
fig:equational-conditions presents equational conditions to define varieties of pointed commutative residuated lattices and
\cref
fig:equational-logics presents equational logics in the usual sense. Let
A
be a single pointed commutative residuated
lattice and
Fm
be the formula algebra for the language of pointed commutative residuated lattices. Given any variety
\mathbb{V}
of pointed commutative residuated lattices, a
logic

\textnormal{{L}}^{\mathbb{V}}
may be defined by fixing, for all
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\subseteq\mathbf{Fm}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash_{\textnormal{{L}}^{\mathbb{V}}}\varphi\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\text{ for any }\mathbf{A}\in\mathbb{V}\text{ and homomorphism }v\colon\mathbf{Fm}\rightarrow\mathbf{A}:

\displaystyle\text{ if for all }\psi\in\Gamma,\quad 1\leqslant v(\psi),\quad\text{ then }\quad 1\leqslant v(\varphi)

By
\mathcal{V}(\mathbb{K})
, we mean the variety generated by a class of pointed commutative residuated lattices
\mathbb{K}
.
Let us define some fundamental fuzzy logics. For any

, let
{L}_{n}=\{0,\frac{1}{n-1},\dots,\frac{n-2}{n-1},1\}
and

\mathbb{L}_{n}=\langle L_{n},min,max,*_{\text{\L}},\to_{\text{\L}},1,0\rangle\quad,\quad\mathbb{G}_{n}=\langle L_{n},min,max,min,\to_{G},1,0\rangle

where
x*_{\text{\L}}y=max\{0,x+y-1\}
,
x\to_{\text{\L}}y=min\{1,1-x+y\}

x\to_{G}y
is
y
if

, otherwise 1. Define

). Separately, define the relevance logic

as the logic of the variety of distributive pointed commutative residuated lattices satisfying

and
\neg\neg x=x
for any

\ \ \ \begin{array}[]{ll}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~Lett.}&1\leq(x\rightarrow y)\vee(y\rightarrow x)\\
\textnormal{({dis})}&x\wedge(y\vee z)=(x\wedge y)\vee(x\wedge z)\\

Regarding the UIP, we have the following result. Our theorem generalizes the results of

[

Corollary 55

(Positive results, UIP)
.

Logics
\mathsf{CFL}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\mathsf{CFL}_{\mathsf{ew}}
, and
{\sf CPC}
, their
K
and
D
modal versions, as well as logics
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{ew}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{ew}}\textnormal{{\sf K}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\textnormal{{\sf D}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{ew}}\textnormal{{\sf D}}
,
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{e}}\textnormal{{\sf T}}
, and
\mathsf{FL}_{\mathsf{ew}}\textnormal{{\sf T}}
have the UIP.

Proof.

The rules of the usual calculi of these logics are semi-analytic, and their axioms are focused, and in the absence of the contraction rule, the calculi are clearly fully terminating.
∎

4.2.3
Non-normal Modal and Conditional Logics

It is possible to extend the methodology to cover (non-normal) modal logics, conditional logics, and intuitionistic modal logics. As a witness to the generality of the methodology, we yet again apply it to a wide range of logics and this time we prove the ULIP. First, we need to introduce these logics.

Set
\mathcal{L}_{\triangleright}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{\wedge,\vee,\to,\bot,\triangleright\}
as the language of conditional logics. For non-normal modal and conditional logics, the property U(L)IP is less explored than in the setting of normal logics. Recall that the basic non-normal modal logic
\mathsf{E}
(resp. the conditional logic
\mathsf{CE}
) is the smallest set of
\mathcal{L}_{\Box}
-formulas (resp.
\mathcal{L}_{\triangleright}
-formulas) containing classical tautologies, closed under (
MP
) and
(
E
)
(resp.
(
CE
)
). Define the other non-normal modal and conditional logics as in
\cref
figModalHilbert, by adding the axioms in
\cref
figaxioms. Also, recall the well-known intuitionistic non-normal modal logic, namely the intuitionistic monotone modal logic
\mathsf{iM}
, which is axiomatized over
{\sf IPC}
by the rule
(
E
)
and the axiom
\Box(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\Box\varphi\wedge\Box\psi
.

Modal axioms:

(
M
)

\displaystyle\ \ \Box(\varphi\wedge\psi)\rightarrow\Box\varphi\wedge\Box\psi

(
C
)

\displaystyle\ \ \Box\varphi\wedge\Box\psi\rightarrow\Box(\varphi\wedge\psi)

(
N
)

\displaystyle\ \ \Box\top

Conditional axioms:

(
CM
)

\displaystyle\ \ (\varphi\triangleright\psi\wedge\theta)\rightarrow(\varphi\triangleright\psi)\wedge(\varphi\triangleright\theta)

(
CN
)

\displaystyle\ \ \varphi\triangleright\top

(
ID
)

\displaystyle\ \ \varphi\triangleright\varphi

(
CC
)

\displaystyle\ \ (\varphi\triangleright\psi)\wedge(\varphi\triangleright\theta)\rightarrow(\varphi\triangleright\psi\wedge\theta)

(
CEM
)

\displaystyle\ \ (\varphi\triangleright\psi)\vee(\varphi\triangleright\neg\psi)

Modal and conditional rules:

\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

(
E
)

\Box\varphi\leftrightarrow\Box\psi

\varphi_{0}\leftrightarrow\varphi_{1}

\psi_{0}\leftrightarrow\psi_{1}

(
CE
)

\varphi_{0}\triangleright\psi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{1}\triangleright\psi_{1}
